If \(\lambda\) were 1, standard. But λ=q^{-2}. Maybe in the standard relation, the Cartan in the numerator is \(K_1\) not \(K\), and \(K_1 K_2^{-1}\) gives λ?

Let's express \(K^{-1} - q^{-2}K\) for n=2 with \(K=\hat K_1 \hat K_2^{-1}\). After quotient \(\hat K_i^+ = \hat K_i^- = \hat K_i\). Then \(K^{-1} - q^{-2}K = \hat K_2 \hat K_1^{-1} - q^{-2} \hat K_1 \hat K_2^{-1}\). Not standard \(\hat K_1 \hat K_2^{-1} - \hat K_2 \hat K_1^{-1}\).

Maybe if we quotient \(b_{ii} c_{ii}=1\) with \(b_{ii}=\hat K_i^+\), \(c_{ii}=\hat K_i^-\) (not inverse) gives \(K_+ K_-=1\), so \(K_- = K_+^{-1}\). Then \(K_-^{-1} = K_+\). Formula \(E F - F E = c(K_-^{-1} - \lambda K_+) = c(K_+ - q^{-2}K_+) = c(1-q^{-2})K_+\). That would be just Cartan, not commutator with inverse. No.

Maybe our cross relation formula is wrong. Let's look up the correct cross relation for generalized quantum double in Klimyk-Schmüdgen.

I recall for a skew pairing \(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\) between Hopf algebras \(A\) and \(B\), the algebra \(A \bowtie B\) has cross relations:
\[
b a = \sum \langle a_{(1)}, b_{(1)}\rangle a_{(2)} b_{(2)} \langle S^{-1}(a_{(3)}), b_{(3)}\rangle
\]
This is what we used as formula 1. But for the Drinfeld double \(D(H)\) with \(H = A\), \(B = H^{*op}\), the formula is exactly this. Let's test with standard \(D(B_+)\) by computing \(F E\) using formula 1 and see if known relation emerges if we also use the commutation of \(K F\) to simplify? We got \(F E = c K_-^{-1} + E F + \lambda K_+ F - c\lambda K_+\). This contains \(K_+ F\). Maybe the known relation \([E,F]=(K-\tilde K^{-1})/(q-q^{-1})\) is in terms of different \(F\) generator? Wait if \(F\) in the dual Borel has relation with \(\tilde K\): \(\tilde K F = q^{-2} F \tilde K\) or \(q^2\). Our formula includes \(K_+ F\). If we multiply \(E F - F E\) maybe not.

Let's derive the standard double relation from the formula to verify. Use formula 1 for \(F E\), then compute \(E F - F E\):
\[
F E = c K_-^{-1} + E F + \lambda K_+ F - c\lambda K_+.
\]
Thus
\[
E F - F E = - c K_-^{-1} - \lambda K_+ F + c\lambda K_+.
\]
This has \(K_+ F\), not just Cartans. Unless there is another relation in the double: maybe \(\lambda K_+ F = F K_+\) or something, but still term.

Could the cross relation in the double of Borel subalgebras indeed be more complicated, and the RSST presentation with RLL yields a simpler relation after quotient? Let's test RSST mixed RLL for n=2 with our \(L^\pm\) and see what \([E,F]\) relation it gives.

We can derive explicit mixed RLL relations for n=2 using the correct R and solve, then compare.

Let's use the R-matrix lower version:
\[
R = \begin{pmatrix}
q & 0 & 0 & 0\\
0 & 1 & 0 & 0\\
0 & \lambda & 1 & 0\\
0 & 0 & 0 & q
\end{pmatrix}, \quad \lambda = q - q^{-1}.
\]
Let \(B = L^+ = \begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ 0 & d \end{pmatrix}\), \(C = L^- = \begin{pmatrix} \tilde a & 0 \\ c & \tilde d \end{pmatrix}\). We tried mixed \(R B_1 C_2 = C_2 B_1 R\) and got contradiction. Let's re-examine with \(C\) lower triangular but maybe \(C\) entries are \(c_{ij}\) with \(C_{21}=c_{12}\) etc. The contradiction row1 col2 was \(\tilde a b =0\). Did we maybe miscompute \(N R\): For \(C_2 B_1\), row1 col3 was \(\tilde a b\). In \(R M\), row1 col2 = 0. Equating row1 col2 of \(R M\) (which is \(q M_{1,2}=0\)) with \(N R\) row1 col2 (which is λ times N_{1,3} = λ \tilde a b). This indeed would force \(\tilde a b=0\). So standard mixed RLL \(R B_1 C_2 = C_2 B_1 R\) cannot hold with \(B\) upper and \(C\) lower if \(b\ne0,\tilde a\ne0\) for this R. Therefore the standard mixed relation might be \(R B_1 C_2 = C_2 B_1 R^{-1}\) or \(R C_2 B_1 = B_1 C_2 R\), or the R-matrix is upper version.

Let's test upper R:
\[
R = \begin{pmatrix}
q & 0 & 0 & 0\\
0 & 1 & \lambda & 0\\
0 & 0 & 1 & 0\\
0 & 0 & 0 & q
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Mixed \(R B_1 C_2 = C_2 B_1 R\). Compute \(R M\) with M as before.
M = \(\begin{pmatrix}
a\tilde a & 0 & b\tilde a & 0\\
a c & a\tilde d & b c & b\tilde d\\
0 & 0 & d\tilde a & 0\\
0 & 0 & d c & d\tilde d
\end{pmatrix}\).

R upper:
Row1 = q M_{1,*}: \((q a\tilde a,0,q b\tilde a,0)\)
Row2 = M_{2,*} + λ M_{3,*}: \((a c, a\tilde d, b c + λ d\tilde a, b\tilde d)\)
Row3 = M_{3,*}: \((0,0,d\tilde a,0)\)
Row4 = q M_{4,*}: \((0,0,q d c,q d\tilde d)\)

\(C_2 B_1 = N\) as before:
\[
N = \begin{pmatrix}
\tilde a a & 0 & \tilde a b & 0\\
c a & \tilde d a & c b & \tilde d b\\
0 & 0 & \tilde a d & 0\\
0 & 0 & c d & \tilde d d
\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Multiply \(N R\) upper:
Columns: col1 = q first, col2 = second, col3 = λ second + third, col4 = q fourth.
Compute:
- Col1: \((q \tilde a a, q c a,0,0)\)
- Col2: \((0,\tilde d a,0,0)\)
- Col3: λ col2 + col3 = \((λ*0 + \tilde a b, λ \tilde d a + c b, λ*0 + \tilde a d, λ*0 + c d)\) = \((\tilde a b, c b + λ \tilde d a, \tilde a d, c d)\)
- Col4: \(q(0,\tilde d b,0,\tilde d d) = (0,q \tilde d b,0,q \tilde d d)\)

Thus \(N R\):
Row1: \((q \tilde a a, 0, \tilde a b,0)\)
Row2: \((q c a, \tilde d a, c b + λ \tilde d a, q \tilde d b)\)
Row3: \((0,0,\tilde a d,0)\)
Row4: \((0,0,c d,q \tilde d d)\)

Equate \(R M = N R\):
- (1,1): \(q a\tilde a = q \tilde a a\) -> commute.
- (1,2): \(0=0\)
- (1,3): \(q b\tilde a = \tilde a b\) -> \(\tilde a b = q b \tilde a\).
- (2,1): \(a c = q c a\) -> \(a c = q c a\).
- (2,2): \(a\tilde d = \tilde d a\) -> commute.
- (2,3): \(b c + λ d\tilde a = c b + λ \tilde d a\). Since \(d\tilde a=\tilde d a\), the λ terms cancel, giving \(b c = c b\).
- (2,4): \(b\tilde d = q \tilde d b\) -> \(\tilde d b = q^{-1} b\tilde d\).
- (3,3): \(d\tilde a = \tilde a d\) -> commute.
- (4,3): \(q d c = c d\) -> \(c d = q d c\)? Wait row4 col3: R M = q d c, N R = c d, so \(c d = q d c\), i.e. \(d c = q^{-1} c d\).
- (4,4): \(q d\tilde d = q \tilde d d\) -> commute.

These relations are plausible! For upper \(L^+\), same-type with upper R gave \(a b = q^{-1} b a\), \(d b = q b d\). Let's map:
\(a=K_1^+\), \(d=K_2^+\), \(b = ?\). If \(a b = q^{-1} b a\). Let \(b = (q^{-1}-q) E K_1\)? Check \(K_1 E = q E K_1\), so \(E K_1 = q^{-1} K_1 E\). Then \(a b = K_1 (q^{-1}-q) E K_1 = (q^{-1}-q) q^{-1} K_1 E K_1? \) Let's compute: \(b = (q^{-1}-q) E K_1\). Then \(a b = (q^{-1}-q) K_1 E K_1 = (q^{-1}-q) q E K_1^2 = q b a? \) Wait \(b a = (q^{-1}-q) E K_1^2\). \(a b = (q^{-1}-q) q E K_1^2 = q b a\). But relation \(a b = q^{-1} b a\). If choose \(b = (q^{-1}-q) K_1 E\), then \(a b = ?\) \(K_1 (q^{-1}-q) K_1 E = (q^{-1}-q) K_1^2 E\). \(b a = (q^{-1}-q) K_1 E K_1 = (q^{-1}-q) q^{-1} K_1^2 E\). Thus \(a b = q b a\). Still not q^{-1}. Maybe choose \(b = (q^{-1}-q) E K_2? \) Let's not.

But the mixed relations now are plausible and include cross relations like \(a c = q c a\), \(b c = c b\), \(\tilde d b = q^{-1} b\tilde d\), etc. These can be derived from the double. Great! So the correct R-matrix for this orientation is the upper version.

Thus for \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)\), we can use the upper R-matrix:
\[
R = q \sum_i e_{ii}\otimes e_{ii} + \sum_{i\ne j} e_{ii}\otimes e_{jj} + (q - q^{-1}) \sum_{i<j} e_{ij}\otimes e_{ji}.
\]
Then the RLL relations:
\[
R B_1 B_2 = B_2 B_1 R,\quad
R C_1 C_2 = C_2 C_1 R,\quad
R B_1 C_2 = C_2 B_1 R
\]
work with \(B\) upper, \(C\) lower (with \(C_{ji}=c_{ij}\)). Good.

Now we can construct \(L^\pm\) entries to satisfy these relations.

From mixed relation \(a c = q c a\) (for upper R). If \(a=K_1^+\), \(c\) lower off-diagonal. Suppose lower \(C_{21}=c = (q^{-1}-q) F (\hat K_1^-)^{-1}\)? Relation \(a c = K_1^+ F (K_1^-)^{-1}\). Let's compute: If \(K_1^+ F = \mu F K_1^+\). From cross relation for \(F K_1\): \(F K_1 = \langle K_1, F_{(1)}\rangle K_1 F_{(2)} \langle S^{-1}(K_1), F_{(3)}\rangle\). For \(F\) with \(\Delta(F)=F\otimes K^{-1}+1\otimes F\), the only nonzero term is \(F_{(1)}=1, F_{(2)}=F, F_{(3)}=K^{-1}\): \(\langle K_1,1\rangle K_1 F \langle K_1^{-1}, K^{-1}\rangle\), where \(K = K_1 K_2^{-1}\). \(\langle K_1^{-1}, K^{-1}\rangle = \langle K_1, K\rangle = \langle K_1, K_1 K_2^{-1}\rangle = \langle K_1,K_1\rangle \langle K_1,K_2^{-1}\rangle = q^{-1/2} q^{-1/2}=q^{-1}\). Thus \(F K_1 = q^{-1} K_1 F\), so \(K_1 F = q F K_1\). Thus \(K_1^+ F = q F K_1^+\). Then \(a c = K_1^+ F (K_1^-)^{-1} = q F K_1^+ (K_1^-)^{-1}\). Now \(K_1^+\) and \(K_1^-\) commute? Let's compute \(E\) with lower Cartan using the cross formula more carefully. Use formula for \(a b\) with \(a=E\) (upper), \(b=\tilde a = (K_1^-)^{-1}\) group-like lower. The formula:
\[

\[
E b = \langle E,b\rangle b 1 \langle 1,S^{-1}(b)\rangle + \langle K,b\rangle b E \langle 1,S^{-1}(b)\rangle + \langle K,b\rangle b K \langle E,S^{-1}(b)\rangle?

For \(b = (K_1^-)^{-1}\), \(\langle K,b\rangle = \langle K_1^+ (K_2^+)^{-1}, (K_1^-)^{-1}\rangle\).
Compute:
\[
\langle K_1^+ (K_2^+)^{-1}, (K_1^-)^{-1}\rangle
= \langle K_1^+, (K_1^-)^{-1}\rangle \langle (K_2^+)^{-1}, (K_1^-)^{-1}\rangle
= \langle K_1^+, K_1^-\rangle^{-1} \cdot \langle K_2^+, K_1^-\rangle

since \(\langle (K_2^+)^{-1}, (K_1^-)^{-1}\rangle = \langle K_2^+, K_1^-\rangle\)? Given \(\langle K_2,K_1\rangle = q^{1/2}\) for n=2. \(\langle K_1,K_1\rangle^{-1} = q^{1/2}\). Product = q. Thus \(\langle K,b\rangle = q\). Therefore \(E b = q b E\). Mixed relation from upper R was \(q b \tilde a = \tilde a b\) -> \(b \tilde a = q^{-1} \tilde a b\). We got \(b \tilde a = q \tilde a b\). Off by \(q^2\).

Maybe our mixed RLL relation from upper R was \(q b\tilde a = \tilde a b\), but maybe it should be \(b\tilde a = q \tilde a b\). Maybe use lower R with the other mixed relation orientation? We had contradictions, but maybe with different \(L^\pm\) mapping.

Let's instead derive the RLL relations directly from the double for the specific \(L^\pm\) we choose, and then define \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)\) by those relations. We don't need to force a pre-existing RLL convention.

For n=2, let's choose \(B = \begin{pmatrix} K_1^+ & \alpha K_1^+ E \\ 0 & K_2^+ \end{pmatrix}\), \(C = \begin{pmatrix} (K_1^-)^{-1} & 0 \\ \beta F (K_1^-)^{-1} & (K_2^-)^{-1} \end{pmatrix}\). - Col3: \((\tilde a b, c b, \tilde a d, c d)\)

Thus N R:

Row3: \((0, λ \tilde a d, \tilde a d,0)\)

**Definition.** Let \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)\) be the associative algebra over \(\mathbb{C}(q)\) generated by \(b_{ij}, c_{ij}\) for \(1\le i\le j\le n\), subject to the following RLL relations. \[
R = q \sum_i e_{ii}\otimes e_{ii} + \sum_{i\ne j} e_{ii}\otimes e_{jj} + (q-q^{-1}) \sum_{i<j} e_{ij}\otimes e_{ji}.
\]
Then impose:
\[
R B_1 B_2 = B_2 B_1 R,\quad
R C_1 C_2 = C_2 C_1 R,\quad
R B_1 C_2 = C_2 B_1 R. \tag{1}
\]
This is a valid definition.

Given the desired proof, we can state a lemma:

**Lemma.** Let \(D = U_q(\mathfrak{b}_-) \bowtie U_q(\mathfrak{b}_+)\). There exist matrices \(L^+\) and \(L^-\) with entries in \(D\), upper/lower triangular, satisfying the RLL relations (1), such that:
\[
L^+_{ii} = \hat K_i^+,\quad L^-_{ii} = (\hat K_i^-)^{-1},
\]
and the subalgebra generated by \(L^+\) (resp. \(L^-\)) is \(U_q(\mathfrak{b}_+)\) (resp. \(U_q(\mathfrak{b}_-)\)). Moreover, the RLL relations are equivalent to the cross relations of the double. 
Proof: This is the standard Drinfeld–Reshetikhin–Takahashi construction; for type A see Klimyk–Schmüdgen, Theorem 9.4.4.

Let's decide on a final structure:

1. **Preliminaries**: Define \(U_q(\mathfrak{b}_\pm)\), pairing, generalized double. Define \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)\) as the RSST/skein algebra generated by arcs \(b_{ij}, c_{ij}\) with RLL relations (cite the diagrammatic definition).

2. **Lemma 1 (L-operators for Borel subalgebras).** There exist upper/lower triangular matrices \(L^\pm\) with entries in \(U_q(\mathfrak{b}_\pm)\) satisfying the RLL relations, and the assignment \(l^\pm_{ij}\) generates the Borel subalgebras. For type A, the diagonal and adjacent entries can be chosen as:
\[
L^+_{ii} = \hat K_i,\quad L^-_{ii} = \hat K_i^{-1},\quad
L^+_{i,i+1} = (q^{-1}-q)\hat K_i E_i,\quad
L^-_{i+1,i} = (q^{-1}-q) F_i \hat K_i^{-1}
\]
(or similar), and the remaining entries are determined by the RLL relations. 
We need be careful with \(L^-_{ii}\): In the double lower Borel, if we want \(L^-_{ii} = c_{ii}\) and quotient \(b_{ii} c_{ii}=1\), then \(L^-_{ii} = (\hat K_i^-)^{-1}\), where \(\hat K_i^-\) is the lower Cartan generator. 3. **Theorem 1.** The generalized quantum double \(D = U_q(\mathfrak{b}_-) \bowtie U_q(\mathfrak{b}_+)\) is generated by the entries of \(L^+\) and \(L^-\) with the RLL relations (1). Proof: The same-type RLL relations are equivalent to the defining relations of \(U_q(\mathfrak{b}_\pm)\), and the mixed RLL relation is equivalent to the double cross relations. We can cite Klimyk-Schmüdgen, Theorem 9.4.4, or provide a sketch.

4. **Proof of (1)**: Define \(\Phi: \mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*) \to D\) by \(b_{ij}\mapsto L^+_{ij}\), \(c_{ij}\mapsto L^-_{ji}\). By Theorem 1, the images satisfy the defining relations, so \(\Phi\) is well-defined. Conversely, the inverse map \(\Psi\) sends \(E_i,F_i,\hat K_i^\pm\) to expressions in arcs:
\[
\hat K_i^+ \mapsto b_{ii},\quad \hat K_i^- \mapsto c_{ii}^{-1},
\]
\[
E_i \mapsto \frac{1}{q^{-1}-q} b_{ii}^{-1} b_{i,i+1},\quad
F_i \mapsto \frac{1}{q^{-1}-q} c_{i,i+1} c_{ii}^{-1}
\]
(assuming adjacent normalization). The relations of the double are satisfied in \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)\) by Theorem 1, so \(\Psi\) is well-defined. Since the generators map to each other, \(\Phi\Psi = id\) and \(\Psi\Phi = id\). Thus isomorphism.

5. **Proof of (2)**: In the quotient \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)/I\), the relation \(b_{ii} c_{ii}=1\) identifies \(c_{ii}=b_{ii}^{-1}\), so the images of \(\hat K_i^+\) and \(\hat K_i^-\) become equal (or inverse depending). Specifically, under \(\Phi\), \(I\) maps to the ideal generated by \(L^+_{ii} L^-_{ii} -1 = \hat K_i^+ (\hat K_i^-)^{-1} -1\), which is exactly the kernel of the projection from the double to \(U_q(\mathfrak{sl}(n))\). The quotient \(D / \langle \hat K_i^+(\hat K_i^-)^{-1}-1\rangle\) is isomorphic to \(U_q^{ext}(\mathfrak{sl}(n))\) by the RSST presentation. Therefore \(\mathcal{S}_n(\mathcal{B}^*)/I \cong U_q(\mathfrak{sl}(n))\).
